Autobidding Agents for Inference Auctions (Section 4). Inference users typically submit many <sub>reques</sub>t<sub>s over</sub> ti<sub>me an</sub>d <sub>mus</sub>t <sub>manage</sub> th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>erence</sub> b<sub>u</sub>d<sub>ge</sub>t<sub>.</sub> E<sub>ven w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> t<sub>ru</sub>thf<sub>u</sub>l <sub>repor</sub>ti<sub>ng</sub> i<sub>s</sub> <sub>op</sub>ti<sub>ma</sub>l i<sub>n eac</sub>h <sub>auc</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> i<sub>n</sub> i<sub>so</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on, users s</sub>till <sub>nee</sub>d t<sub>o c</sub>h<sub>oose</sub> h<sub>ow aggress</sub>i<sub>ve</sub>l<sub>y</sub> t<sub>o</sub> bid <sub>across</sub> <sub>reques</sub>t<sub>s so</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>erence</sub> b<sub>u</sub>d<sub>ge</sub>t i<sub>s spen</sub>t <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> it i<sub>s mos</sub>t <sub>va</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>bl<sub>e.</sub> T<sub>o a</sub>dd<sub>ress</sub> thi<sub>s, we</sub> design an autobidding agent that adjusts a user’s bids over time to maximize their cumulative utility, subject to a budget constraint. ## 2 Cache-Optimal Schedules and Our Bidding Environment

Serving an autoregressive language model has two stages. During prefill, the model processes a request’s prompt and stores its keys and values in the KV cache. Definition 2.3 (Inference Auction). Given a radix tree T, an inference auction consists of (1) a scheduling rule $A : \mathbb { R } _ { + } ^ { n } \to { \mathcal { D } } ( { \mathsf { T } } )$ mapping bids to DFS schedules and (2) a payment rule $p : \mathbb { R } _ { + } ^ { n } \to \mathbb { R } _ { + } ^ { m }$ ma<sub>pp</sub><sup>i</sup>n<sub>g</sub> <sup>bid</sup>s to <sub>p</sub>a<sub>y</sub>ments, w<sup>h</sup>ere $p _ { i } ( { \bf { b } } _ { 1 : m } )$ i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>paymen</sub>t <sub>c</sub>h<sub>arge</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> <sub>user</sub> �<sub>.</sub>

Any inference auction enjoys optimal cache utilization by Theorem 3.1 of Zheng et al. [52].

Let $h _ { i } ( \mathbf { b } _ { - i } ) = \operatorname* { m a x } _ { \sigma \in \mathcal { D } ( \mathbb { T } ) } W \big ( \sigma ; ( 0 , \mathbf { b } _ { - i } ) \big )$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e max</sub>i<sub>mum</sub> <sub>repor</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>we</sub>lf<sub>are</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>o</sub>th<sub>er</sub> <sub>users</sub> <sub>cou</sub>ld <sub>o</sub>bt<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub> if <sub>user</sub> �’<sub>s</sub> bid<sub>s</sub> <sub>were</sub> <sub>se</sub>t t<sub>o</sub> <sub>zero</sub> <sub>w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> it<sub>s</sub> <sub>reques</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>rema</sub>i<sub>n</sub> i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e ra</sub>di<sub>x</sub> t<sub>ree.</sub><sup>4</sup> Th<sub>e</sub> VCG <sub>paymen</sub>t <sub>ru</sub>l<sub>e c</sub>h<sub>arges user</sub> �

1. Eficiency: Under truthful bidding, true welfare $W ( \sigma ; { \mathbf { v } } _ { 1 : m } )$ i<sub>s</sub> <sub>max</sub>i<sub>m</sub>i<sub>ze</sub>d <sub>over</sub> DFS <sub>sc</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d<sub>u</sub>l<sub>es.</sub>

Incentive compatibility (IC): $V _ { i } ( \bar { A } ( \mathbf { v } _ { i } , \mathbf { b } _ { - i } ) ) - p _ { i } ^ { \mathrm { v c g } } ( \mathbf { v } _ { i } , \mathbf { b } _ { - i } ) \geq V _ { i } ( \bar { A } ( \mathbf { b } _ { i } , \mathbf { b } _ { - i } ) ) - p _ { i } ^ { \mathrm { v c g } } ( \mathbf { b } _ { i } , \mathbf { b } _ { - i } )$ f<sub>or</sub> <sub>a</sub>ll ${ \bf v } _ { i } , { \bf b } _ { i } , { \bf b } _ { - i }$ <sub>.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>ma</sub>k<sub>es</sub> t<sub>ru</sub>thf<sub>u</sub>l biddi<sub>ng</sub> <sub>an</sub> <sub>op</sub>ti<sub>ma</sub>l <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>egy.</sub>

3. Individual rationality (IR): $V _ { i } ( \bar { A } ( \mathbf { v } _ { i } , \mathbf { b } _ { - i } ) ) - p _ { i } ^ { \mathtt { V C G } } ( \mathbf { v } _ { i } , \mathbf { b } _ { - i } ) \geq 0$ f<sub>or a</sub>ll $\mathbf { v } _ { i } , \mathbf { b } _ { - i }$

4. No positive transfers: $p _ { i } ^ { \mathtt { V C G } } ( \mathbf { b } _ { 1 : m } ) \geq 0$ f<sub>or a</sub>ll bid <sub>vec</sub>t<sub>ors</sub> $ { \mathbf { b } } _ { 1 : m }$

Eficient Payment Computation. Naively computing VCG payments for each user consists of repeatedly zeroing out that user’s bids and rerunning Algorithm 1. Algorithms. dfs-bid is the average-bid DFS of Algorithm 1 with VCG payments as in Section 3.2. dfs-uniform runs the same DFS with all bids e<sub>q</sub>ual (meant to re<sub>p</sub>licate SGLan<sub>g</sub>’s lon<sub>g</sub>est-<sub>p</sub>refixmatch <sub>p</sub>olic<sub>y</sub>); bid-sort sorts <sub>q</sub>ueries b<sub>y</sub> bid and i<sub>g</sub>nores the <sub>p</sub>refix tree (it is the unconstrained welfare maximizer in Pro<sub>p</sub>osition 2.4); no-sort serves <sub>q</sub>ueries in arrival order. Values are s<sub>y</sub>nthetic, drawn i.i.d. (unless otherwise noted) from 1 + Pareto(1.5), and bids are truthful. ## 6 Related Work

Prefix-Aware LLM Serving. Modern LLM serving frameworks improve request throughput via continuous batchin<sub>g</sub> and eficient mana<sub>g</sub>ement of the KV cache. vLLM [34] uses ideas from virtual memor<sub>y</sub> and <sub>p</sub>a<sub>g</sub>in<sub>g</sub> to reduce KV cache fra<sub>g</sub>mentation. SGLan<sub>g</sub> [52] stores re<sub>q</sub>uests in a radix tree and uses lon<sub>g</sub>est <sub>p</sub>refix matchin<sub>g</sub> to increase cache reuse across re<sub>q</sub>uests. Preble [45] jointl<sub>y</sub> o<sub>p</sub>timizes <sub>p</sub>refix reuse and load balancin<sub>g</sub> across multi<sub>p</sub>le GPUs, while BatchLLM [53] <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s re<sub>q</sub>uests with common <sub>p</sub>refixes ofline and coordinates their execution. Dexter et al. [17] stud<sub>y</sub> o<sub>p</sub>timal <sub>reques</sub>t <sub>sc</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d<sub>u</sub>li<sub>ng</sub> <sub>un</sub>d<sub>er</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>cer</sub>t<sub>a</sub>i<sub>n</sub> <sub>arr</sub>i<sub>va</sub>l <sub>pa</sub>tt<sub>ern</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>en</sub> <sub>cac</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>reques</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ore</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>ra</sub>di<sub>x</sub> t<sub>ree, an</sub>d th<sub>ey propose a new</sub> f<sub>am</sub>il<sub>y o</sub>f <sub>sc</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d<sub>u</sub>li<sub>ng po</sub>li<sub>c</sub>i<sub>es w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erpo</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween</sub> fi<sub>rs</sub>t<sub>-come-</sub> first-served and lon<sub>g</sub>est <sub>p</sub>refix matchin<sub>g</sub>. Llumnix [46] and QoServe [22] su<sub>pp</sub>ort diferentiated latency objectives and request prioritization. Another related line of work studies fairness in LLM servin<sub>g</sub> [11, 44]. ## 7 Conclusion

We design the first inference auction, a mechanism for allocating LLM serving capacity during times of hi<sub>g</sub>h re<sub>q</sub>uest volume. Process called on the root of T visits every node once, and every entry of the hash map $Z _ { \mathsf { c } _ { j } }$ is visited. Each call to LocalExternality(�, A<sub>u</sub>[�]) incurs $O ( | \mathcal { A } _ { \mathsf { u } } [ i ] | \log d ( \mathsf { u } ) )$ <sub>run-</sub>ti<sub>me.</sub> S<sub>o,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>o</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l <sub>run-</sub>ti<sub>me across a</sub>ll <sub>ca</sub>ll<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o</sub> L<sub>ocal</sub>E<sub>xternality</sub> i<sub>s</sub> $O ( \sum _ { \mathbf { u } } \sum _ { i } | \mathcal { A } _ { \mathbf { u } } [ i ] | \log d ( \mathbf { u } ) ) \leq O ( \Gamma \log \nu )$

For each non-root node u and each entr<sub>y</sub> $( i , z ) \in Z _ { \mathbf { u } _ { \perp } }$ <sub>c</sub>h<sub>arge</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>en</sub>t<sub>ry</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>an</sub> <sub>ar</sub>bit<sub>rary</sub> <sub>pos</sub>iti<sub>ve-</sub>bid <sub>reques</sub>t $r \in \mathcal { R } _ { i } \cap \mathcal { L } ( \mathbf { u } )$ . We have therefore <sub>p</sub>roven Theorem 3.3.

Theorem 3.3.
